Spring4Shell extends to Glassfish and Payara: same vulnerability, new exploit

You’ve heard of Spring4Shell, now learn about similar exploits for Glassfish and Payara that leverage the same issue in Spring, but with a different payload.

Spring4Shell classLoader property injection via dirContext.docBase (Glassfish/Payara/Tomcat) AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound HTTP requests carrying the Spring4Shell data-binding payload that writes to a ClassLoader-reachable property, specifically the Glassfish/Payara variant 'class.module.classLoader.resources.dirContext.docBase=/' described by Snyk. Fires on the literal classLoader/docBase tokens an attacker must send to reset the app's document root and reach the filesystem. Rationale: Anchored on the exact attacker-controlled tokens from the article: 'class.module.classLoader' and the Payara/Glassfish-specific 'class.module.classLoader.resources.dirContext.docBase'. These strings have no legitimate use in normal request URIs/bodies. Cross-checked against Microsoft's SpringShell guidance, Unit42, and Citrix WAF rule 999004 which all key on '.classLoader' in the request. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/spring4shell-rce-vulnerability-glassfish-payara/ • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2022/04/04/springshell-rce-vulnerability-guidance-for-protecting-against-and-detecting-cve-2022-22965/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cve-2022-22965-springshell/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authenticated vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Qualys/Tenable) firing CVE-2022-22965 plugins against the app fleet
  • Internal red-team or sanctioned pentest replaying the Snyk PoC
  • Security researchers / bug-bounty hunters probing internet-facing Spring apps
  • WAF in detect-only mode logging blocked-elsewhere probes that never reached the origin
  • Synthetic monitoring or DAST tooling (e.g. OWASP ZAP) in CI/CD scanning staging endpoints
Suppression filter
| where UA !has "Nessus" and UA !has "qualys" and UA !has "zaproxy" and UA !has "Nuclei" and SrcIp !in ("10.0.0.0/8 scanner ranges go here")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the request reached the origin (status 200/302 vs WAF 403) and whether the target app runs vulnerable spring-beans (< 5.3.18 / < 5.2.20) on Tomcat/Glassfish/Payara. Pull all requests from the same SrcIp in the surrounding hour. 2) CONTAIN: If the request was served (2xx) and the host is vulnerable, block the source IP at the WAF/edge, snapshot the app server, and patch spring-beans to 5.3.18/5.2.20+. 3) HUNT: Pivot to UC 'Spring4Shell arbitrary file read' on the same host/SrcIp for follow-on /etc/passwd or WEB-INF retrieval, and review app logs for written webshell/JSP artifacts under the (now possibly relocated) docBase.
let lookback = 7d;
union isfuzzy=true
(W3CIISLog | where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback) | project TimeGenerated, SrcIp = cIP, Method = csMethod, Uri = csUriStem, Query = csUriQuery, UA = csUserAgent, Status = tostring(scStatus), Host = Computer, LogSrc = "W3CIISLog"),
(AppServiceHTTPLogs | where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback) | project TimeGenerated, SrcIp = CIp, Method = CsMethod, Uri = CsUriStem, Query = CsUriQuery, UA = UserAgent, Status = tostring(ScStatus), Host = CsHost, LogSrc = "AppServiceHTTPLogs"),
(CommonSecurityLog | where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback) | project TimeGenerated, SrcIp = SourceIP, Method = RequestMethod, Uri = RequestURL, Query = RequestContext, UA = RequestClientApplication, Status = tostring(EventOutcome), Host = DestinationHostName, LogSrc = "CommonSecurityLog")
| where Uri has "class.module.classLoader" or Query has "class.module.classLoader" or Uri has "dirContext.docBase" or Query has "dirContext.docBase"
| summarize Hits = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), SampleUri = any(Uri), SampleQuery = any(Query) by SrcIp, Host, Method, UA, LogSrc
| order by LastSeen desc
title: Spring4Shell classLoader Property Injection (Glassfish/Payara/Tomcat)
id: 3f9c2a1e-7b4d-4e2a-9c1f-8a6b5d4e3c2b
status: experimental
description: Detects inbound web requests containing the Spring4Shell data-binding payload that writes a ClassLoader-reachable property, including the Glassfish/Payara dirContext.docBase variant (CVE-2022-22965).
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/spring4shell-rce-vulnerability-glassfish-payara/
  - https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2022/04/04/springshell-rce-vulnerability-guidance-for-protecting-against-and-detecting-cve-2022-22965/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.initial_access
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection_query:
    cs-uri-query|contains:
      - 'class.module.classLoader'
      - 'dirContext.docBase'
  selection_uri:
    c-uri|contains:
      - 'class.module.classLoader'
      - 'dirContext.docBase'
  condition: selection_query or selection_uri
falsepositives:
  - Authenticated vulnerability scanners (Nessus, Qualys, Burp) probing for CVE-2022-22965
  - Internal red-team / pentest exercises
level: high

| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`

Spring4Shell post-exploitation arbitrary file read (/etc/passwd via relocated docBase) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the second stage of the Snyk Glassfish/Payara exploit: after docBase is reset to '/', the attacker fetches absolute filesystem paths through the deployed app context (e.g. /handling-form-submission-complete/etc/passwd). Fires on web requests whose URI ends in sensitive filesystem paths served by a Java app server, plus an EDR variant for the app-server process opening /etc/passwd. Rationale: Uses the exact follow-on request shape from the article — the deployed context '/handling-form-submission-complete/' concatenated with absolute path '/etc/passwd' — generalised to the sensitive-file tokens an attacker reads once docBase is '/'. The Falcon variant pivots to the app-server (java/payara/glassfish) process opening /etc/passwd, catching the file read even if the web tier isn't logged. Confirmed via Snyk's published PoC by Calum Hutton. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/spring4shell-rce-vulnerability-glassfish-payara/ • https://blog.payara.fish/payara-and-spring4shell
ATT&CKT1083 T1003.008
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • DAST / path-traversal scanners (Burp, ZAP, Nuclei LFI templates) requesting /etc/passwd as a canary
  • Legitimate static-content apps whose URL path coincidentally contains '/etc/' segments
  • Backup or config-management tooling on the host reading /etc/passwd via the same Java service account (Falcon variant)
  • Container image build/health probes that cat /etc/passwd during startup
  • Security posture tools enumerating WEB-INF/web.xml for inventory
Suppression filter
| where UA !has "Nuclei" and UA !has "zaproxy" and UA !has "Nessus" and Status startswith "2"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Check the HTTP status — a 200 on a URI ending in /etc/passwd from a Java app context is near-certain compromise; correlate with a preceding dirContext.docBase POST from the same SrcIp (UC 1). On the EDR side, confirm the java/payara process actually opened /etc/passwd. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the app server, block the SrcIp, rotate any credentials/secrets readable from the now-exposed filesystem, and patch spring-beans to 5.3.18/5.2.20+. 3) HUNT: Enumerate every distinct absolute path the SrcIp requested (config files, keystores, /WEB-INF/, SSH keys) to scope data disclosure, and check for webshell drops under the relocated docBase.
let lookback = 7d;
union isfuzzy=true
(W3CIISLog | where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback) | project TimeGenerated, SrcIp = cIP, Method = csMethod, Uri = csUriStem, UA = csUserAgent, Status = tostring(scStatus), Host = Computer, LogSrc = "W3CIISLog"),
(AppServiceHTTPLogs | where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback) | project TimeGenerated, SrcIp = CIp, Method = CsMethod, Uri = CsUriStem, UA = UserAgent, Status = tostring(ScStatus), Host = CsHost, LogSrc = "AppServiceHTTPLogs"),
(CommonSecurityLog | where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback) | project TimeGenerated, SrcIp = SourceIP, Method = RequestMethod, Uri = RequestURL, UA = RequestClientApplication, Status = tostring(EventOutcome), Host = DestinationHostName, LogSrc = "CommonSecurityLog")
| where Uri has "/etc/passwd" or Uri has "/etc/shadow" or Uri has "/etc/hosts" or Uri has "/WEB-INF/web.xml"
| summarize Hits = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), SampleUri = any(Uri) by SrcIp, Host, Method, UA, Status, LogSrc
| order by LastSeen desc
title: Spring4Shell Post-Exploitation Arbitrary File Read via Relocated docBase
id: b7d1e4c9-2f6a-4c83-9a55-1e0c7b2d8f41
status: experimental
description: Detects web requests retrieving absolute filesystem paths (e.g. /etc/passwd) through a Java app-server context after a Spring4Shell dirContext.docBase reset (CVE-2022-22965, Glassfish/Payara variant).
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/spring4shell-rce-vulnerability-glassfish-payara/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.t1083
  - attack.t1003.008
  - attack.credential_access
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection:
    c-uri|contains:
      - '/etc/passwd'
      - '/etc/shadow'
      - '/etc/hosts'
      - '/WEB-INF/web.xml'
  condition: selection
falsepositives:
  - Path-traversal vulnerability scanners testing for arbitrary file read
  - Legitimate apps that genuinely expose a resource path literally containing these tokens
level: high
#event_simpleName=FileOpenInfo TargetFileName=/\/etc\/(passwd|shadow)$/i
| ContextImageFileName=/(java|payara|glassfish|tomcat)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, TargetFileName], function=count())
| count > 0
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime

// Entra ID directory audit — user / admin consent to a third-party app.
AuditLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OperationName has "Consent to application"
| where Result =~ "success"
| extend AppName = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].displayName),
         AppId   = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].id),
         User    = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName)
| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc

``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Exploring 3 types of directory traversal vulnerabilities in C/C++

Snyk
2022-04-04 4 use cases 1 technique 4 kill-chain phases detected

Learn how directory traversal vulnerabilities work on web servers written on C/C++, as well as how to prevent them, including arbitrary file read & write and the zip slip vulnerability.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Web path-traversal arbitrary file read via '../' in static-file URL (Crow CVE-2021-23514)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Arbitrary file write via '../' in upload 'name' parameter (Mongoose CVE-2022-25299) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Exploring 3 types of directory traversal vulnerabilities in C/C++
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Web path-traversal arbitrary file read via '../' in static-file URL (Crow CVE-2021-23514) AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound HTTP requests whose URL contains '../' (or URL-encoded equivalents) combined with sensitive read targets like /etc/passwd, SSH keys or .env, matching the Crow C++ framework arbitrary-file-read PoC (curl --path-as-is /static/../../etc/passwd). Targets exploitation of unsanitised static-file serving in C/C++ web servers. Rationale: Anchored on the article's exact PoC: '../' traversal in a /static/ URL reaching /etc/passwd (Crow). Pairing the traversal token with sensitive-file targets keeps FP low versus matching '..' alone. Cross-checked Snyk CVE-2021-23514 advisory and the original blog. Cross-checked against: • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-UNMANAGED-CROW-2336163 • https://snyk.io/blog/exploring-3-types-of-directory-traversal-vulnerabilities-in-c-c/
ATT&CKT1190 T1083
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorised vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Nikto/Burp) probing for traversal during assessment windows
  • Web crawlers following malformed relative links that collapse to '../'
  • SPA/router URLs that legitimately carry '..' fragments in query strings
  • Legitimate filenames containing double-dots (e.g. report..final.pdf) requested from /static/
  • Penetration testers using curl --path-as-is during scheduled engagements
Suppression filter
| where RequestClientApplication !has_any ("Nessus","Nikto","sqlmap","Burp")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full RequestURL + SourceIP and the HTTP status — a 200/206 on a '../etc/passwd' request means the read succeeded; 404/403 means blocked. Check if SourceIP belongs to an authorised scanner. 2) Contain: if a sensitive file was served (2xx), block the SourceIP at the WAF/edge and rotate any credentials/keys reachable from the web root (SSH keys, .env secrets, app config). 3) Hunt: pivot on the SourceIP for other traversal attempts and successful 2xx reads across all web hosts; confirm the web framework version (Crow < v0.3+4 is vulnerable) and patch.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DeviceProduct in~ ("WAF","Web Application Firewall") or isnotempty(RequestURL)
| where RequestURL has_any ("../", "..%2f", "%2e%2e%2f", "%2e%2e/")
| where RequestURL has_any ("/static/", "etc/passwd", "/.ssh/", "id_rsa", ".env", "../../")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct
| sort by TimeGenerated desc
title: Web Path Traversal to Sensitive File via Static-File URL

Arbitrary file write via '../' in upload 'name' parameter (Mongoose CVE-2022-25299) AIInstallation High
Hunts POST requests to /upload whose query/body 'name' parameter contains '../' traversal, matching the Mongoose embedded-server arbitrary-file-write PoC (/upload?offset=0&name=../malicious-file) that drops a file outside the intended /tmp folder. Includes a host-side companion that surfaces the resulting file written to the filesystem root. Rationale: Anchored on the Mongoose PoC: POST /upload with name=../ (CVE-2022-25299) writing 'malicious-file' to filesystem root. The web-log query catches the exploitation request; the Defender/FDR file-write-at-root queries catch the successful outcome (file landing in '/' outside the intended /tmp). Cross-checked Snyk and cvedetails CVE-2022-25299. Cross-checked against: • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-UNMANAGED-CESANTAMONGOOSE-2404180 • https://www.cvedetails.com/cve/CVE-2022-25299
ATT&CKT1190 T1505.003
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Pentesters/scanners fuzzing the upload endpoint with traversal payloads during authorised tests
  • Legitimate uploads whose original filename contains double-dots (e.g. v1..2.bin) passed through to name=
  • Backup/sync agents POSTing to a /upload API with relative path names
  • QA automation exercising upload input validation
  • Admin tooling that writes config files directly under '/' on minimal embedded/container images
Suppression filter
| where RequestClientApplication !has_any ("Nessus","Nikto","sqlmap","Burp")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the POST returned a 2xx and correlate the SourceIP/host with the DeviceFileEvents/FDR file-write-at-root hit on the same server within minutes — a matching write means the exploit landed. Inspect the written file's contents/extension (web shell, overwritten /etc/init.d script, or replaced server binary). 2) Contain: block the SourceIP at the edge, quarantine/delete the malicious file, and isolate the host if a system file or executable was overwritten (potential RCE). 3) Hunt: enumerate all files recently created outside the intended upload dir by the web-server process, verify Mongoose < 7.6, and check for follow-on execution of the dropped file.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "FileCreated"
| where FolderPath == "/"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !in~ ("system")
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith "/usr/" and InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith "/sbin/" and InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith "/bin/"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| sort by Timestamp desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestMethod =~ "POST"
| where RequestURL has "/upload"
| where RequestURL has_any ("name=..", "name=%2e%2e", "name=../", "name=..%2f")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct
| sort by TimeGenerated desc

| where (FolderPath has_any ("/etc/passwd", "/tmp/test.txt", "/etc/init.d/", "/tmp/path_to_the_content_root_directory/"))
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1486 T1566

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```

| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd

              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;

          ParentProcessName

title: Office Application Spawning Script Host
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d07
status: experimental
description: |
    Word/Excel/Outlook/OneNote spawning powershell, cmd, wscript,
    cscript, mshta, rundll32, regsvr32 — classic macro / embedded-
    object payload signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059

    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\winword.exe'
            - '\excel.exe'
            - '\powerpnt.exe'
            - '\outlook.exe'

            - '\mspub.exe'
            - '\visio.exe'

            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high
ENDPOINT

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:

            - '\psexec.exe'

            - '\paexec.exe'

            - '\remcom.exe'

            - '\wmic.exe'

            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high

// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent

| where Account !endswith "$"

   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"

Critical

Spring4Shell: The zero-day RCE in the Spring Framework explained

Snyk
2022-04-01 5 use cases 2 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

On March 30, 2022, a critical remote code execution (RCE) vulnerability was found in the Spring Framework. This vulnerability is another example of why securing the software supply chain is important to open source.

CVEsCVE-2022-22965
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Weaponization

Detected

Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Spring4Shell (CVE-2022-22965) classLoader pipeline injection / JSP webshell call in web logs · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Tomcat/Java process writing a .jsp webshell into webapps (Spring4Shell drop)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Tomcat/Java JVM spawning command shell (Spring4Shell webshell command execution)
Spring4Shell (CVE-2022-22965) classLoader pipeline injection / JSP webshell call in web logs AIExploitation High
Hunts the Spring4Shell exploitation payload that manipulates Tomcat's access-log valve via the magic 'class.module.classLoader.resources.context.parent.pipeline.first.*' Spring data-binding properties, and the follow-on GET to the dropped JSP webshell (e.g. tomcatwar.jsp?pwd=j&cmd=whoami). Article-specific because it anchors on the exact property path and webshell query-string shape, not generic RCE. Rationale: Anchors on the verbatim 'class.module.classLoader...pipeline.first' property chain from the article's curl PoC plus the article's own webshell URL shape (rce.jsp / tomcatwar.jsp?pwd=j&cmd=whoami). Cross-checked against Microsoft, Unit42 and Trend Micro which confirm the same classLoader pipeline tokens and JSP-with-cmd= access pattern. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2022/04/04/springshell-rce-vulnerability-guidance-for-protecting-against-and-detecting-cve-2022-22965/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cve-2022-22965-springshell/ • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/22/d/cve-2022-22965-analyzing-the-exploitation-of-spring4shell-vulner.html
ATT&CKT1190 T1505.003
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authenticated vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Qualys/Tenable) firing Spring4Shell check payloads
  • Authorized penetration tests or bug-bounty automation replaying the public PoC
  • WAF/security-control validation testing against staging Tomcat
  • Internet background-noise mass scanners hitting the endpoint with 4xx responses (no actual vulnerable controller)
Suppression filter
| where not (csUserAgent has_any ("Nessus","Qualys","Nuclei","masscan","zgrab"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full request for the matching cIP/Url and check scStatus — a 200 on a POST to a Spring controller followed by a successful GET to the same .jsp is exploitation; repeated 4xx with no .jsp follow-up is scanning. Confirm the host runs Spring on JDK9+. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF/edge, isolate the Tomcat host, and remove any rce.jsp/tomcatwar.jsp/shell.jsp under webapps. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC2/UC3 on the same host to confirm JSP file drops and java-spawned shells; verify spring-beans is patched to 5.3.18/5.2.20 (Boot 2.6.6/2.5.12).
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where (csUriQuery has_any ("class.module.classLoader","classLoader.resources","pipeline.first.pattern","pipeline.first.suffix","pipeline.first.directory"))
    or (csUriStem endswith ".jsp" and csUriQuery has "cmd=" and csUriQuery has "pwd=")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, AttackerIP = cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Tomcat/Java process writing a .jsp webshell into webapps (Spring4Shell drop) AIInstallation High
Hunts the file-system artifact of Spring4Shell: the Tomcat JVM writing a new .jsp file (rce.jsp, tomcatwar.jsp, shell.jsp) into a webapps directory via the abused access-log valve. High-fidelity because a running Tomcat process should never author new JSP source under webapps at runtime. Rationale: Uses the article's concrete artifacts — rce.jsp written to webapps/myapp, plus the well-corroborated tomcatwar.jsp/shell.jsp drops — and the fact that the writer is the Tomcat JVM (java). Microsoft and Wazuh both flag Tomcat-process JSP creation under webapps as the canonical Spring4Shell file IOC. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2022/04/04/springshell-rce-vulnerability-guidance-for-protecting-against-and-detecting-cve-2022-22965/ • https://wazuh.com/blog/detecting-spring4shell-cve-2022-22965-with-wazuh/
ATT&CKT1505.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate WAR auto-deploy/hot-deploy expanding an application archive that contains JSPs into webapps
  • Developer or CI/CD pipeline pushing a new JSP to a dev/staging Tomcat
  • Tomcat manager-app application redeploy during a maintenance window
  • Backup/restore tooling rehydrating a webapps directory
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("org.apache.catalina.startup.HostConfig","deployWAR","autoDeploy"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the dropped file (FolderPath/FileName) and inspect contents — Runtime.getRuntime().exec, request.getParameter("cmd"), or out.println("HACKED") confirm a webshell vs a legit app JSP. Check whether the write coincided with an app deployment event. 2) Contain: quarantine the JSP, isolate the host, and capture the file for forensics before deletion. 3) Hunt: correlate with UC1 (the inbound classLoader payload) and UC3 (java spawning shells) on the same DeviceName within minutes; sweep all webapps dirs on peer Tomcat hosts for the same filenames/hashes.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName endswith ".jsp"
| where FolderPath has "webapps"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("java.exe","java","tomcat","catalina") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("catalina","tomcat","org.apache.catalina")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType == "FileCreated"
| where TargetFileName endswith ".jsp"
| where TargetFilePath has "webapps"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("java","tomcat","catalina")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Tomcat/Java JVM spawning command shell (Spring4Shell webshell command execution) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts post-exploitation command execution where the Tomcat JVM (java/catalina) spawns cmd.exe, powershell, /bin/sh or recon utilities like whoami — exactly the Runtime.exec() behaviour the article's tomcatwar.jsp webshell uses (whoami via ?cmd=). Java web servers spawning interactive shells is rare and high-signal. Rationale: Built on the article's explicit execution detail — the webshell runs whoami via Runtime.exec() (tomcatwar.jsp?cmd=whoami) — so it pivots on the java/catalina parent spawning shells/recon binaries. Unit42 and Microsoft both document Tomcat spawning anomalous child processes as the Spring4Shell execution signal. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cve-2022-22965-springshell/ • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2022/04/04/springshell-rce-vulnerability-guidance-for-protecting-against-and-detecting-cve-2022-22965/
ATT&CKT1059.003 T1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Jenkins/Gradle/Maven build agents (themselves Java) legitimately spawning sh/cmd for build steps
  • Application code that shells out to system utilities (git, ImageMagick, ffmpeg, pdf tools) via Runtime.exec
  • Java-based monitoring/management agents running ipconfig/whoami for inventory
  • Tomcat lifecycle scripts (catalina.sh) invoking shell during start/stop
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("jenkins","gradle","maven","sonar","bamboo-agent","buildAgent")) and not (DeviceName has_any ("build","ci-","jenkins"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: review the child ProcessCommandLine — interactive recon (whoami, net user, ipconfig, id) or download (curl/wget to an external host) from an internet-facing Tomcat is malicious; build-pipeline shelling on a CI host is benign. Confirm the host serves Spring on JDK9+. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the java-spawned process tree, block any outbound IP it contacted, and preserve the JSP under webapps. 3) Hunt: tie back to UC1 (inbound classLoader payload) and UC2 (JSP drop) on the same host; sweep for lateral movement, new local accounts, and persistence created under the java-spawned shell's session.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("java.exe","java") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("catalina","org.apache.catalina","tomcat")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","whoami.exe","net.exe","net1.exe","ipconfig.exe","sh","bash","whoami","id","curl","wget")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("java","tomcat","catalina")
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","whoami","net.exe","net1.exe","ipconfig.exe","bash","sh","curl","wget","id")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, User = TargetUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-22965")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-22965")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

| sort - severity

    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities
    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2022-22965")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve

Critical

Spring4Shell: What we know about the Java RCE vulnerability

Snyk
2022-03-31 6 use cases 1 technique 4 kill-chain phases detected

Spring4Shell or SpringShell is a credible RCE vulnerability in spring-beans package, which is part of Spring Core. This is a key enabler of the inversion of control (IoC) capabilities of Spring. This is often referred to as dependency injection.

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Spring4Shell (CVE-2022-22965) exploit: class.module.classLoader ClassLoader manipulation in HTTP request · [LLM] Java/Tomcat spawns command shell or download tool (Spring4Shell RCE execution)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Tomcat/Java writes JSP webshell into webapps (Spring4Shell post-exploit drop)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Outbound connection to Spring4Shell Mirai staging IPs (107.174.133.167 / 194.31.98.186) · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Spring4Shell (CVE-2022-22965) exploit: class.module.classLoader ClassLoader manipulation in HTTP request AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound web requests carrying the Spring4Shell data-binding payload that pivots through 'class.module.classLoader.resources.context.parent.pipeline' to reconfigure Tomcat's AccessLogValve and write a JSP webshell. This is the single most specific network-layer signature of CVE-2022-22965 exploitation against a Tomcat WAR deployment. Rationale: Anchors on the exact Spring4Shell exploit token 'class.module.classLoader.resources.context.parent.pipeline' used to repurpose Tomcat's AccessLogValve — a string that never appears in legitimate traffic. Confirmed by Microsoft, Unit42 and Huntress write-ups. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2022/04/04/springshell-rce-vulnerability-guidance-for-protecting-against-and-detecting-cve-2022-22965/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cve-2022-22965-springshell/ • https://www.huntress.com/threat-library/vulnerabilities/spring4shell
ATT&CKT1190 T1505.003
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorised vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Qualys/Snyk) probing for Spring4Shell
  • Internal red-team / pentest exercises against Spring apps
  • Security researchers replaying public PoC against a staging host
  • WAF in detect-only mode logging blocked-but-recorded exploit strings
Suppression filter
| where RequestClientApplication !has_any ("Nessus", "Qualys", "Nuclei", "zgrab")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the destination is a Java/Tomcat WAR app and whether it responded 200 with body; pull the full RequestURL/body and source IP. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF/edge, isolate the host if a 2xx response was returned, and snapshot the Tomcat webapps/ROOT directory for forensics. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC2/UC3 on the same host for a freshly written .jsp and any java-spawned shell, and verify Spring is patched to 5.2.20/5.3.18 (Boot 2.5.12/2.6.6).
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestURL has "class.module.classLoader" or RequestURL has "classLoader.resources.context.parent.pipeline" or RequestContext has "class.module.classLoader"
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationIP, DestinationHostName, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Tomcat/Java writes JSP webshell into webapps (Spring4Shell post-exploit drop) AIInstallation High
Detects a Java/Tomcat process writing a .jsp/.jspx file into a Tomcat webapps/ROOT directory — the file-system footprint of a successful Spring4Shell ClassLoader webshell drop (e.g. tomcatwar.jsp, shell.jsp). Catches exploitation even when the inbound payload was missed at the network layer. Rationale: Combines the article's Tomcat-9+/Spring requirement with the corroborated webshell artefact (.jsp dropped into webapps/ROOT, e.g. tomcatwar.jsp) and ties it to a java/catalina parent, making a benign .jsp deploy distinguishable from an exploit-driven write. Cross-checked against: • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/22/d/cve-2022-22965-analyzing-the-exploitation-of-spring4shell-vulner.html • https://www.huntress.com/threat-library/vulnerabilities/spring4shell
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate WAR/app redeployment auto-extracting JSPs into webapps
  • CI/CD pipeline hot-deploying a new JSP page during a release
  • Tomcat manager-app deploying an application
  • Developer copying a JSP into ROOT during local troubleshooting
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("org.apache.catalina.startup.Bootstrap") and TargetFilePath has "work/Catalina")
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the written .jsp and check for Runtime.exec/ProcessBuilder/getParameter webshell content and an unusual recent mtime versus the rest of the deployment. 2) Contain: quarantine the file, remove world-write on webapps/ROOT, and isolate the host if the file is a confirmed shell. 3) Hunt: correlate to UC1 (inbound class.module.classLoader request) and UC3 (java spawning a shell) on the same host within the surrounding minutes, and review access logs for subsequent GET/POST to the new .jsp path.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName endswith ".jsp" or FileName endswith ".jspx"
| where (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("java.exe","java","tomcat9.exe","tomcat8.exe")) or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("tomcat","catalina")
| where FolderPath has_any ("webapps","\\ROOT\\","/ROOT/","tomcat")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Java/Tomcat spawns command shell or download tool (Spring4Shell RCE execution) AIExploitation Medium
Detects a Java/Tomcat process spawning an interactive shell or download utility (cmd, powershell, sh/bash, curl, wget, whoami, python) — the execution signal of a Spring4Shell webshell or direct RCE running attacker commands. Java servers very rarely fork these binaries in normal operation. Rationale: Pairs the article's hard precondition (RCE only on JRE 9+ AND Tomcat 9+) with the parent→child relationship every public Spring4Shell PoC and the Trend Micro Mirai chain produce: the Java/Catalina process forking a shell or wget/curl to stage payloads. Cross-checked against: • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/22/d/cve-2022-22965-analyzing-the-exploitation-of-spring4shell-vulner.html • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cve-2022-22965-springshell/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1190 T1505.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Java app legitimately shelling out to ImageMagick/ffmpeg/git via ProcessBuilder
  • Tomcat health-check or backup scripts invoking sh/curl
  • CI build agents running on a JVM that fork shells
  • Application calling whoami/uname for environment introspection at startup
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("ffmpeg","convert","identify","git ") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the child ProcessCommandLine — benign apps shell out with stable, repeatable args; exploitation shows recon (whoami/id/uname) or wget/curl to an external IP. 2) Contain: kill the offending child tree and isolate the host if recon or external download is present. 3) Hunt: pivot on UC4 for connections to the Mirai staging IPs, search the same host for new .jsp files (UC2), and review the parent JVM's recent inbound web requests for UC1's ClassLoader payload.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("java.exe","java","tomcat9.exe","tomcat8.exe")) or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("org.apache.catalina","catalina.startup.Bootstrap")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","sh","bash","dash","curl","wget","whoami","whoami.exe","python","python3","perl")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Outbound connection to Spring4Shell Mirai staging IPs (107.174.133.167 / 194.31.98.186) AICommand & Control Medium
Detects any host connecting to the two IPs tied to the Spring4Shell-driven Mirai campaign: 194.31.98.186 (exploitation source / payload host) and 107.174.133.167 (hosted the t.sh dropper pulled into /tmp). A hit indicates either inbound exploitation or successful second-stage payload retrieval. Rationale: Uses the two campaign IPs that two sources (Trend Micro + the article's IOC list) both attribute to Spring4Shell Mirai activity — 194.31.98.186 as the exploitation/payload source and 107.174.133.167 as the t.sh dropper host. The unconfirmed domain test6.ggdd.co.uk was deliberately excluded. Cross-checked against: • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/22/d/cve-2022-22965-analyzing-the-exploitation-of-spring4shell-vulner.html • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cve-2022-22965-springshell/
ATT&CKT1105 T1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IP reassigned to benign hosting years after the 2022 campaign (verify infra age / WHOIS before alerting)
  • Threat-intel platform or sandbox detonating samples that beacon to these IPs
  • Researcher reproducing the campaign in a lab
  • Shared-hosting reuse where the IP now serves unrelated content
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the local process owning the connection (InitiatingProcessFileName/ContextImageFileName) — wget/curl/sh from a Java context is high-confidence; confirm the IP is still attacker-controlled via current TI. 2) Contain: block both IPs at the egress firewall and isolate any internet-facing Tomcat host that initiated outbound to them. 3) Hunt: on the connecting host, search /tmp for a dropped t.sh and Mirai binary, check UC2/UC3 for the webshell and java-spawned wget, and sweep all Spring/Tomcat assets for the same IOC connections.

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

How Snyk helps satisfy White House cybersecurity recommendations

ATT&CKT1190 T1566 T1566.001
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Protestware is trending in open source: 4 different types and their impact

Snyk
2022-03-22 5 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

At Snyk, we’d like to help the community reach a consensus on how to approach the various protestware springing into existence, and help differentiate between the different types.

CVEsCVE-2022-23812
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] node-ipc destructive payload artifact (dao/ssl-geospec.js) on disk
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] node-ipc/peacenotwar geo-locate check via ipgeolocation.io from Node runtime
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] peacenotwar protestware desktop file drop (WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt)
peacenotwar protestware desktop file drop (WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt) AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the signature artifact of the peacenotwar npm package (pulled in by node-ipc >=11.0.0) which writes a file named WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt to the user's Desktop/OneDrive folders during install. This is the article's 'Out-of-env protest log' type 3 made concrete — a near-zero-FP filename string. Rationale: Uses the exact unique filename 'WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt' confirmed by Snyk's peacenotwar advisory and the GitHub Security Advisory GHSA-97m3-w2cp-4xx6 — a string with no benign software use, making it alerting-grade. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/peacenotwar-malicious-npm-node-ipc-package-vulnerability/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-97m3-w2cp-4xx6 • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2022-23812
ATT&CKT1491.001 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A security researcher deliberately installs node-ipc/peacenotwar in a sandbox to analyze it
  • A developer clones a repo whose test fixtures include a copy of the marker file
  • Backup/restore of an old workstation that was infected in March 2022 re-lands the file
  • A malware-analysis training course that distributes the sample
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("7z.exe","7zg.exe","winrar.exe","explorer.exe")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the FolderPath (Desktop/OneDrive) and InitiatingProcessFileName — a node.exe/npm parent during an install window is true-positive; an archive-extraction tool or analyst sandbox is likely benign. Pull the host's package-lock.json / node_modules for node-ipc version. 2) CONTAIN: If node-ipc >=11.0.0 or peacenotwar is present in a build/dev host, quarantine the affected node_modules, pin node-ipc to a safe version, and rotate any secrets touched by that build. 3) HUNT: Sweep all dev/CI hosts for the same filename and for ssl-geospec.js (UC3) and ipgeolocation.io callbacks (UC2) to scope the dependency's blast radius.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc

node-ipc/peacenotwar geo-locate check via ipgeolocation.io from Node runtime AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the shared 'geo-locate via IP/timezone' vector the article describes across es5-ext, peacenotwar and node-ipc: a Node process reaching out to ipgeolocation.io during/after package install to decide whether to fire protest behaviour. Pivots on the node.exe initiator + the specific geo service domain. Rationale: Combines the article's 'geo-locate via time zone/IP' vector with the externally-confirmed concrete service (ipgeolocation.io, per StepSecurity/Orca analysis of ssl-geospec.js) and constrains to a node.exe initiator to separate protestware from generic browsing. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/node-ipc-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://orca.security/resources/blog/cve-2022-23812-protestware-malicious-code-node-ipc-npm-package/
ATT&CKT1614 T1195.002
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An app legitimately using a geo-IP npm library (e.g. node-ipgeolocation) that calls the same API
  • A developer testing a geolocation feature locally during the time window
  • An analytics or licensing SDK that performs IP-geo lookups at startup
  • A CI job that exercises geo-aware code paths
  • Internal QA using ipgeolocation.io free tier for legitimate enrichment
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm","install","node-ipc","postinstall")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Inspect InitiatingProcessCommandLine/FolderPath — is node running an npm install/postinstall, or a long-lived legit app? Check package-lock for node-ipc (>=11 or 10.1.1/10.1.2) and peacenotwar. 2) CONTAIN: If a vulnerable node-ipc/peacenotwar version is found on a dev/CI host, isolate node_modules, block ipgeolocation.io egress for build agents, and pin to a safe node-ipc release. 3) HUNT: On the same host pivot to UC1 (WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt) and UC3 (ssl-geospec.js) file artifacts, and check for mass file-modification bursts by node.exe indicating the destructive payload fired.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has "ipgeolocation.io"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImDnsActivity
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DnsQuery has "ipgeolocation.io"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, DnsQuery, DnsResponseName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

node-ipc destructive payload artifact (dao/ssl-geospec.js) on disk AIInstallation High
Hunts the malicious file dao/ssl-geospec.js that node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 added — the base64-obfuscated module that geo-checks and then overwrites files with a heart emoji (CVE-2022-23812). Rationale: Uses the exact malicious artifact path 'dao/ssl-geospec.js' confirmed by NVD CVE-2022-23812 and StepSecurity/Socket analyses; the filename scoped to the node-ipc package folder is unique to the sabotaged 10.1.1/10.1.2 releases, giving alerting fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2022-23812 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-NODEIPC-2426370 • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/node-ipc-npm-supply-chain-attack
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1485
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A malware researcher intentionally installs node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 in a lab for analysis
  • An offline npm mirror or cached node_modules retains the compromised version
  • A pinned legacy dependency in an old test repo re-installs the bad version
  • A forensic image being mounted/indexed on an analysis workstation surfaces the file
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the parent node-ipc package version from package.json/package-lock in the same folder; 10.1.1/10.1.2 (or 11.x importing peacenotwar) = true positive. Determine whether the host's egress IP geolocates to Russia/Belarus, which is what arms the wipe. 2) CONTAIN: Immediately isolate the host if it geolocates to RU/BY (destructive trigger) or shows mass file-modification by node.exe; remove the package, restore overwritten files from backup, and rotate credentials present on the host. 3) HUNT: Enterprise-wide sweep for ssl-geospec.js, WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt (UC1), and ipgeolocation.io callbacks (UC2); audit CI/CD pipelines and lockfiles for the pinned malicious versions across all repos.

dompdf security alert: RCE vulnerability found in popular PHP PDF library

Snyk
2022-03-18 5 use cases 1 technique 4 kill-chain phases detected

A major RCE vulnerability has been identified in PHP library dompdf. Code can be loaded into an application and then remotely executed whilst a PDF is being generated.
