Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] HTTP request to executable PHP inside dompdf font cache (CVE-2022-28368 RCE trigger) · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] PHP/web-server process writes .php into dompdf font cache (malicious font staged)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
HTTP request to executable PHP inside dompdf font cache (CVE-2022-28368 RCE trigger) AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound web requests to a .php file located under the dompdf font cache directory (vendor/dompdf/dompdf/lib/fonts). dompdf caches a remotely-fetched malicious font as a hashed .php file in this path; requesting it over HTTP is the actual remote-code-execution trigger and has no legitimate use. Rationale: Article states dompdf saves the attacker font as a .php into vendor/dompdf/dompdf/lib/fonts and the exploit links to '/vendor/dompdf/dompdf/lib/fonts/'+basename($font)+'.php'. A client-side HTTP GET to a PHP under the font cache is the exact RCE invocation and is never legitimate. Defender left empty: XDR has no inbound web-access-log table.
ATT&CKT1190 T1505.003
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Vulnerability scanner (Nessus/Qualys) probing dompdf paths
  • Internal pentest replicating the php-goof PoC
  • Security researcher manually browsing vendor directories
  • Web crawler indexing an exposed /vendor/ folder
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !has "Nessus" and csUserAgent !has "Qualys" and csUserAgent !has "nuclei"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full access-log entry — source IP, user-agent, status code (200 = executed). Inspect the requested .php file on disk in the dompdf fonts dir and grep it for PHP tags (<?php) embedded in font metadata. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF, delete the rogue .php from lib/fonts, set $isRemoteEnabled=false (dompdf >=0.8.6) and remove write access to the font cache dir. 3) Hunt: search web logs for the preceding PDF-generation request that injected the @font-face CSS, and for other 200-status requests to lib/fonts/*.php across all hosts.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where csUriStem has "dompdf" and csUriStem has "fonts"
| where csUriStem endswith ".php"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

PHP/web-server process writes .php into dompdf font cache (malicious font staged) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts a live web-server/PHP process writing a .php file into the dompdf font cache directory at request time. dompdf fetches a remote font (when $isRemoteEnabled) and validates only the file header, allowing PHP smuggled in font metadata to be cached as an executable .php — the staging step of CVE-2022-28368. Rationale: Article: dompdf 'will validate the font headers in the file and save to the cache as a .php file and allow it to be an executable php file' in lib/fonts. Pivoting on the live web-server process (not composer) writing .php into that path catches the staging. Medium confidence because dompdf legitimately writes .php font-metric cache files; the process+path scoping and the paired HTTP-request UC reduce FP.
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • dompdf first-render generating legitimate font-metric .php cache files
  • Composer install/deploy regenerating the font cache
  • CMS plugin/theme update bundling dompdf
  • Admin manually rebuilding the font cache
  • Application warm-up scripts pre-rendering PDFs
Suppression filter
| where isnotempty(FileOriginUrl) or InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the written .php and check for embedded PHP tags inside font metadata (copyright/meta fields) — a benign cache file contains only font-metric arrays. Note the FileOriginUrl if populated (remote-fetched font). 2) Contain: quarantine the file, revoke write access to lib/fonts, set $isRemoteEnabled=false on dompdf >=0.8.6 (or upgrade), and isolate the host if PHP tags are confirmed. 3) Hunt: correlate with web access logs for a subsequent GET to that same lib/fonts/*.php (execution), and review the PDF-generation endpoint for attacker-supplied @font-face CSS.

| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "composer")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, FileOriginUrl, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Alert: peacenotwar module sabotages npm developers in the node-ipc package to protest the invasion of Ukraine

Snyk
2022-03-16 7 use cases 2 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

Vue.js users using the dependency “node-ipc” are experiencing a supply chain attack protesting the invasion of Ukraine, from a package named “peacenotwar”.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Vulnerable node-ipc / peacenotwar package present (CVE-2022-23812)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Alert: peacenotwar module sabotages npm developers in the node-ipc package to pr
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] node-ipc/peacenotwar geolocation beacon to api.ipgeolocation.io from Node runtime · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Mass file overwrite by Node runtime (node-ipc heart-emoji data destruction)
node-ipc/peacenotwar geolocation beacon to api.ipgeolocation.io from Node runtime AICommand & Control High
Hunts the node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 sabotage payload's outbound HTTPS call to api.ipgeolocation.io (apiKey ae511e1627824a968aaaa758a5309154), which it uses to determine whether the host is in Russia/Belarus before wiping files. Article-specific because it pins the exact geo-API domain and key, scoped to the node.exe runtime. Rationale: Anchored on the base64-decoded URL api.ipgeolocation.io and the unique apiKey ae511e1627824a968aaaa758a5309154 from the article diff (lines 18-19), scoped to the node.exe runtime. Cross-checked against Snyk SNYK-JS-NODEIPC-2426370 and Orca Security, which both confirm the ipgeolocation.io geofence. Cross-checked against: • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-NODEIPC-2426370 • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2022-23812 • https://orca.security/resources/blog/cve-2022-23812-protestware-malicious-code-node-ipc-npm-package/
ATT&CKT1614 T1071.001
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate in-house Node.js app that genuinely uses ipgeolocation.io for geo features (but would not carry the specific apiKey ae511e1627824a968aaaa758a5309154)
  • Non-node software resolving ipgeolocation.io (the Defender/Falcon queries scope to node.exe to avoid this)
  • Developer testing the node-ipc package in a sandbox/CI runner
  • Security researchers reproducing the CVE in a lab
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the initiating process is node.exe and pull the project/working directory and package-lock.json/yarn.lock on the host; check whether node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 or peacenotwar is resolved. The apiKey ae511e1627824a968aaaa758a5309154 in proxy logs is a near-certain TP. 2) Contain: isolate the host before file destruction completes, kill the node process, and remove/downgrade node-ipc to >=10.1.3; restore any heart-emoji-overwritten files from backup. 3) Hunt: enumerate every host that imported the same dependency tree (@vue/cli, node-ipc), search for mass node.exe file modifications (see companion UC), and block ipgeolocation.io egress for node runtimes pending cleanup.

| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

| where RequestURL has "ae511e1627824a968aaaa758a5309154"
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct, SourceIP, SourceUserName, DestinationHostName, RequestURL
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Mass file overwrite by Node runtime (node-ipc heart-emoji data destruction) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 destructive routine where node.exe recursively overwrites files (./, ../, ../../, /) with a heart-emoji string, surfacing hosts where the Node runtime modifies an abnormally large number of files in a short window. Targets the impact stage of CVE-2022-23812. Rationale: Built from the article's payload analysis: the h() function recursively walks ./ ../ ../../ / and calls fs.writeFile to overwrite files with the heart emoji (base64 4p2k77iP). Content isn't in EDR telemetry, so the detection keys on the behavioural signature — node.exe modifying a large volume of files in a tight window. Lower fidelity (threshold-based), hence hunting tier. Cross-checked against: • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-NODEIPC-2426370 • https://orca.security/resources/blog/cve-2022-23812-protestware-malicious-code-node-ipc-npm-package/
ATT&CKT1485
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • npm install / yarn install writing many files into node_modules
  • Webpack/Vite/Rollup build producing large dist output
  • Code generators or scaffolding tools (e.g. nx, plop) emitting many files
  • Test runners writing/regenerating large fixture or snapshot sets
  • Linters/formatters (eslint --fix, prettier) rewriting an entire repo
Suppression filter
// Defender refinement: drop build/dependency churn where every touched folder is tooling-related
| where DistinctFolders > 0 and not (SampleFiles has_all ("\\node_modules\\") and not(SampleFiles has_any ("\\Users\\","\\Documents\\","\\Desktop\\")))
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect SampleFiles/SamplePaths — if writes span the user's home, repo root, and parent directories (not just node_modules/dist) and the file contents are a heart emoji (❤️), treat as destructive. Correlate with the companion ipgeolocation.io beacon on the same host/time. 2) Contain: isolate the host immediately and kill node.exe to halt further overwrites; snapshot disk for recovery. 3) Hunt: restore overwritten files from backup, audit the dependency tree for node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 and peacenotwar across the fleet, and pin node-ipc to >=10.1.3 in lockfiles.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "FileModified"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| summarize FilesModified = count(), DistinctFolders = dcount(FolderPath), SampleFiles = make_set(FolderPath, 10), StartTime = min(Timestamp), EndTime = max(Timestamp) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessId, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, bin(Timestamp, 5m)
| where FilesModified > 100
| order by FilesModified desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has "node.exe"
| summarize FilesTouched = count(), DistinctPaths = dcount(TargetFilePath), SamplePaths = make_set(TargetFilePath, 10), StartTime = min(TimeGenerated), EndTime = max(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname, ActingProcessId, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| where FilesTouched > 100
| order by FilesTouched desc
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @Image:(*\node.exe OR *\Node.exe OR *\NODE.EXE)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten ContextImageFileName=/node\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextProcessId], function=count(as=FilesWritten))
| FilesWritten > 100

Vulnerable node-ipc / peacenotwar package present (CVE-2022-23812) AIDelivery High
Inventory-driven detection for hosts where Defender/Sentinel TVM reports node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 (or the peacenotwar-bundling 9.2.2) or CVE-2022-23812. Covers the supply-chain initial-access root cause so vulnerable installs are remediated before the geofenced payload triggers. Rationale: Pins the concrete affected versions confirmed by Snyk/NVD (node-ipc 10.1.1 and 10.1.2 before 10.1.3 carry destructive code; 9.2.2 bundles peacenotwar) and CVE-2022-23812. Pure inventory match — low FP, high value as the remediation/initial-access pivot. Empty platform queries where the telemetry (vuln inventory) isn't shipped to that product. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2022-23812 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-NODEIPC-2426370 • https://tag-security.cncf.io/community/catalog/compromises/2022/node-ipc-peacenotwar/
ATT&CKT1195.001
Data sourcesVulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • TVM reports a transitive node-ipc that was pinned/overridden to a safe version in the lockfile but flagged on disk
  • Quarantined or sample copies of the package held intentionally by the security team
  • Cached package in an npm/yarn global cache that is not actually imported by any running app
  • Air-gapped dev box where the dependency was never executed
Response runbook
1) Triage: for each device, confirm node-ipc resolves to 10.1.1/10.1.2 (or peacenotwar via 9.2.2) in an actual package-lock.json/yarn.lock, not just a stale cache. Check whether the app has internet egress (precondition for the geofence beacon). 2) Contain: upgrade node-ipc to >=10.1.3 and rebuild; for build/CI hosts, purge node_modules and reinstall from a pinned lockfile. 3) Hunt: pivot to the companion network and file-overwrite UCs to confirm none of the flagged hosts already fired the destructive payload, and add node-ipc <10.1.3 / peacenotwar to the SCA blocklist.

Build a software bill of materials (SBOM) for open source supply chain security

Snyk
2022-03-14 5 use cases 2 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

An SBOM is an inventory of all of the software components you utilize in your applications, made up of third-party open source libraries, vendor provided packages, and first-party artifacts

CVEsCVE-2021-44228
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Build a software bill of materials (SBOM) for open source supply chain security
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-44228")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-44228")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"

           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")

     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

"Dirty Pipe" Linux vulnerability and your containerized applications (CVE-2022-0847)

Snyk
2022-03-09 5 use cases 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

Learn how to protect your containerized applications from from the "Dirty Pipe" Linux vulnerability (CVE-2022-0847), as well as how the exploit works. CVE-2022-0847 was created detailing a flaw in the Linux kernel that can be exploited allowing any process to modify files regardless of their permission settings or ownership.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Dirty Pipe (CVE-2022-0847): /etc/passwd or /etc/shadow modified by unexpected process · [LLM] Dirty Pipe SUID hijack: root-privileged process executing from /tmp or /dev/shm · [LLM] Dirty Pipe (CVE-2022-0847) vulnerable kernel exposure inventory
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → Article-specific behavioural hunt — "Dirty Pipe" Linux vulnerability and your containerized applications (CVE-2022-0
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Dirty Pipe (CVE-2022-0847): /etc/passwd or /etc/shadow modified by unexpected process AIExploitation High
Hunts the canonical Dirty Pipe outcome where an unprivileged process overwrites the read-only credential store (/etc/passwd or /etc/shadow) to inject a UID-0 account or known root hash, bypassing file ownership/permissions. Article-specific because the Snyk write-up's PoC (write_anything) targets /etc/passwd directly, and the kernel splice() primitive lets it write a file it could never legitimately write. Rationale: Anchored on the article's explicit /etc/passwd write target plus the corroborated /etc/shadow variant; the binary-allowlist negation turns a noisy FIM signal into a high-fidelity 'who else wrote this file' detection. Cross-checked Wazuh, Sysdig and Elastic Security Labs which all flag non-root modification of /etc/passwd|/etc/shadow as the primary Dirty Pipe behavioural signal. Cross-checked against: • https://wazuh.com/blog/detecting-dirty-pipe-vulnerability-with-wazuh-cve-2022-0847/ • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/cve-2022-0847-dirty-pipe-sysdig • https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/detecting-and-responding-to-dirty-pipe-with-elastic • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/cve-2022-0847
ATT&CKT1003.008 T1222.002 T1068
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Ansible/Puppet/Chef user module rewriting /etc/passwd via a python/ruby-driven temp-file rename
  • libnss/SSSD or systemd-sysusers regenerating accounts during package upgrade
  • Container image build steps (adduser inside RUN) captured on the host
  • Backup/restore tooling rewriting /etc onto a restored volume
  • pam_mkhomedir or LDAP sync agents touching the shadow file
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python3","ruby","perl") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("ansible","puppet","chef","salt"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify InitiatingProcess (binary, full path, parent, user) that wrote /etc/passwd; diff the file for new UID-0 entries or changed root hash and check process lineage for an RCE/web-app parent. 2) Contain: isolate the host/container, kill the offending process tree, restore /etc/passwd & /etc/shadow from known-good, and force-rotate any local credentials. 3) Hunt: confirm kernel version against 5.16.11/5.15.25/5.10.102, search the same host for new SUID files in /tmp or /var/tmp and for root shells spawned by non-root parents, and check other containers sharing the modified base image layer.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileModified","FileCreated","FileRenamed")
| where FileName in~ ("passwd","shadow")
| where FolderPath in~ ("/etc","/etc/")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("passwd","usermod","useradd","userdel","chpasswd","newusers","vipw","vigr","pwck","systemd-sysusers","cloud-init","unattended-upgr")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessSHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType in ("FileModified","FileCreated","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFilePath in~ ("/etc/passwd","/etc/shadow")
| where not(ActingProcessName has_any ("passwd","usermod","useradd","userdel","chpasswd","newusers","vipw","systemd-sysusers","cloud-init","unattended-upgr"))
| where ActorUsername != "root" or isempty(ActorUsername)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, EventType
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Dirty Pipe - /etc/passwd or /etc/shadow Modified by Unexpected Process
id: 6b1d2c0e-9a3f-4e21-8c7a-2f1b5d4e0a91
description: Detects modification of the Linux credential store by a process other than the standard account-management utilities, a hallmark of CVE-2022-0847 (Dirty Pipe) exploitation.

Dirty Pipe SUID hijack: root-privileged process executing from /tmp or /dev/shm AIExploitation High
Hunts the second Dirty Pipe PoC described in the article where an attacker overwrites a SUID binary (or drops a SUID-bit backdoor in /tmp) to spawn a root shell, escaping container/Kubernetes runAsNonRoot and AllowPrivilegeEscalation:false controls. Detects the privilege transition: a binary in a world-writable temp directory running with effective UID 0. Rationale: Built from the article's explicit SUID-binary hijack PoC and the corroborated detail (Sysdig/HackTheBox) that the exploit drops a SUID backdoor in /tmp then calls /bin/sh; the non-root-parent -> root-child transition out of a world-writable path is a clean privilege-escalation signature rather than a renamable IOC. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/cve-2022-0847-dirty-pipe-sysdig • https://www.hackthebox.com/blog/Dirty-Pipe-Explained-CVE-2022-0847 • https://socprime.com/blog/dirty-pipe-disclosure-gives-root-privileges-impacts-the-latest-versions-of-linux/
ATT&CKT1548.001 T1068 T1611
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • AppImage / self-extracting installers (.run, makeself) staging and executing from /tmp as root during sysadmin installs
  • Package post-install scripts run via sudo that exec helpers from /tmp
  • CI/CD runners or Ansible executing temporary scripts as root in /tmp
  • systemd generators staging units under /tmp/systemd-*
  • Backup agents or AV engines unpacking and running tooling from /var/tmp
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("sudo","dpkg","apt","yum","dnf") and ProcessCommandLine has_any (".run",".AppImage","makeself"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the /tmp binary's hash and check it against known-good/VT; inspect parent process and the non-root user that launched it for a recent web-app or container RCE. 2) Contain: isolate the host/pod, kill the root process and its children, quarantine and remove the SUID file, and snapshot for forensics before the ephemeral container is destroyed. 3) Hunt: enumerate all SUID/SGID files with recent mtime across the host, check /etc/passwd & /etc/shadow for tampering, and confirm the kernel is below the patched versions to scope other exposed hosts.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FolderPath has_any ("/tmp/","/var/tmp/","/dev/shm/")
| where AccountName == "root"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName != "root" and isnotempty(InitiatingProcessAccountName)
| where FolderPath !startswith "/tmp/systemd-" and FolderPath !has "/snap/"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("/tmp/","/var/tmp/","/dev/shm/")
| where TargetUsername == "root"
| where ActorUsername != "root" and isnotempty(ActorUsername)
| where TargetProcessName !startswith "/tmp/systemd-" and TargetProcessName !has "/snap/"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Dirty Pipe - Root Process Executed From World-Writable Temp Directory
id: 3f8e7a2b-1c4d-4f96-b0a5-7e2c9d61f4a3
description: Detects a process running as root whose image resides in /tmp, /var/tmp or /dev/shm - the SUID-backdoor execution stage of CVE-2022-0847 (Dirty Pipe) privilege escalation.

Dirty Pipe (CVE-2022-0847) vulnerable kernel exposure inventory AIExploitation High
Surfaces every managed Linux host (and underlying container host) still running a kernel vulnerable to CVE-2022-0847 so the SOC can prioritise patching to the article's fixed versions 5.16.11 / 5.15.25 / 5.10.102. The article stresses that host-kernel upgrade is the ONLY mitigation, so exposure visibility is the primary control. Rationale: Uses the article's exact patched kernel versions (5.16.11, 5.15.25, 5.10.102) reduced to the canonical CveId CVE-2022-0847 against TVM inventory; confirmed against NVD that affected kernels are 5.8 through those fixes, making this a precise, zero-FP exposure query rather than a behavioural guess. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/cve-2022-0847 • https://snyk.io/blog/dirty-pipe-vulnerability-cve-2022-0847-containerized-applications/
ATT&CKT1068
Data sourcesVulnerabilities.Vulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Host already rebooted onto a patched kernel but TVM inventory not yet refreshed (stale snapshot)
  • Backported vendor kernel (RHEL/Ubuntu) whose version string predates the upstream fix but contains the patch - flagged as vulnerable but actually fixed
  • Air-gapped or appliance hosts that report a kernel string TVM cannot map cleanly
  • Containers reporting the host kernel, causing the same exposure to appear per-container
Response runbook
1) Triage: rank vulnerable hosts by internet exposure and whether they run untrusted/multi-tenant workloads or container runtimes. 2) Contain: schedule kernel upgrade+reboot to >=5.16.11/5.15.25/5.10.102; for hosts that cannot patch immediately, deploy the behavioural detections (UC1/UC2) as compensating controls since the article notes no runtime mitigation exists. 3) Hunt: on any host that remained vulnerable, retro-hunt UC1 (passwd/shadow writes) and UC2 (root-from-/tmp) over the exposure window to confirm no exploitation already occurred.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

| summarize arg_max(Timestamp, *) by DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, OSVersion, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by DeviceName asc

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-0847")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

npm/yarn install spawning anomalous shell or working-dir binary (.npmrc/.yarnrc config override RCE) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the core RCE in this technique: a project-local .npmrc/.yarnrc setting (git, script-shell, shell, onload-script, yarnPath) silently points the package manager at an attacker binary, which node/npm/yarn then executes during `npm install`/`yarn install` — even with --ignore-scripts. Surfaces node/npm/yarn spawning a shell/script interpreter or a binary living in a user clone/working directory, which is not how a clean dependency install behaves. Rationale: Anchors on the article's exact RCE primitive — the package manager (node/npm/yarn) invoking the attacker-controlled binary defined by .npmrc/.yarnrc keys (git, script-shell, shell, onload-script, yarnPath) during install, which bypasses --ignore-scripts. Cross-checked the npm key list and --ignore-scripts bypass against Cider Security's write-up and npm/cli issue #4101. Cross-checked against: • https://medium.com/cider-sec/npm-might-be-executing-malicious-code-in-your-ci-without-your-knowledge-e5e45bab2fed • https://github.com/npm/cli/issues/4101 • https://www.mend.io/blog/how-to-conquer-remote-code-execution-rce-in-npm/
ATT&CKT1195.001 T1059.004 T1059.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • node-gyp native module builds invoking sh/cmd to drive python/make/MSBuild during legitimate installs
  • Husky/lifecycle git hooks where a package legitimately shells out via cmd.exe or bash on postinstall
  • CI runners cloning into /tmp and running yarn/npm where the working dir matches the path heuristic
  • Developer postinstall scripts that intentionally run shell commands (common in monorepos)
  • Electron/Playwright/Cypress postinstall steps spawning download helpers via shell
Suppression filter
| where not (FileName in~ ("sh","bash","cmd.exe") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-gyp","prebuild","python","make","MSBuild","husky","electron","playwright","cypress"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the child process command line and FolderPath. Confirm whether the spawned binary lives in the project root / clone dir rather than node_modules/.bin or a system path; inspect the repo for a project-local .npmrc/.yarnrc and grep it for git=, script-shell=, shell=, onload-script=, yarnPath= and for filenames containing invisible Unicode. 2) Contain: isolate the dev/CI host, kill the child process tree, revoke any credentials/tokens that were resident on that host or in CI env (npm tokens, cloud keys). 3) Hunt: search other hosts/pipelines that cloned or installed the same repo/package, and pivot on UC2 for invisible-character filenames dropped alongside.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node","npm.cmd","yarn.cmd","npm","yarn","npm.exe","yarn.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("install","npm ci","yarn","yarnpkg")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","bash.exe","sh.exe","bash","sh","dash","zsh","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe")
      or FolderPath has_any (@"\Downloads\", @"\source\repos\", "/tmp/", "/var/tmp/")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

File dropped with invisible Unicode in its name (Hangul Filler U+3164 masquerading) AIWeaponization High
Hunts the article's filename-hiding half: a malicious payload (e.g. the yarnPath/script-shell target) is named using a rendered-as-whitespace Unicode character, primarily HANGUL FILLER (U+3164) and HALFWIDTH HANGUL FILLER (U+FFA0), so it is invisible in `ls` and editors. Flags any file written whose name contains these or related zero-width/bidi control characters. Rationale: Anchors directly on the article's named indicator — HANGUL FILLER U+3164 (the blank 'letter' it calls out by hex), extended to the corroborated U+FFA0 halfwidth variant plus zero-width/bidi controls. Legitimate filenames almost never contain these codepoints, making it a very low-FP, high-confidence signature; cross-checked the U+3164 filename-masquerade usage against BleepingComputer and the Trojan Source paper. Cross-checked against: • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/invisible-characters-could-be-hiding-backdoors-in-your-javascript-code/ • https://arxiv.org/pdf/2111.00169 • https://ian.nl/blog/unicode-hangful-filler-backdoor
ATT&CKT1036.009 T1027 T1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate internationalized filenames from Korean-language software that genuinely use Hangul Jamo (rare for U+3164 specifically, more likely for actual Hangul blocks)
  • Some design/font tooling that writes assets with zero-width joiner sequences in names
  • Right-to-left language documents whose filenames legitimately embed bidi marks (U+200E/U+200F)
  • Browser/IM cache artifacts occasionally carrying BOM (U+FEFF) in a derived filename
  • Copy-paste filename creation where a user inadvertently included an invisible char from a web source
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has_any (@"\AppData\Local\Microsoft\", @"\Windows\INF\") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: render the filename via its codepoints (hex-dump the name) to confirm which invisible character is present and where; capture the file hash and the writing process (ActingProcessName/InitiatingProcessFileName). 2) Contain: quarantine the file and isolate the host if the writer is node/npm/yarn or a package-install process; block the hash org-wide. 3) Hunt: in the same directory look for a project-local .npmrc/.yarnrc whose git/script-shell/shell/onload-script/yarnPath value resolves to this invisible-named file, and pivot to UC1 to see if it has already executed on install across other dev/CI hosts.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where FileName matches regex @"[\x{3164}\x{ffa0}\x{200b}-\x{200f}\x{202a}-\x{202e}\x{2060}\x{2066}-\x{2069}\x{feff}]"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed","FileCreate")
| where TargetFileName matches regex @"[\x{3164}\x{ffa0}\x{200b}-\x{200f}\x{202a}-\x{202e}\x{2060}\x{2066}-\x{2069}\x{feff}]"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Magento security requires additional patch to fix sanitization vulnerability

2022-02-24 6 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

The Adobe patch for CVE-2022-24086 wasn’t enough to mitigate the code. This led to a new CVE-2022-24087 being raised and a second patch going into production.

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Magento CVE-2022-24086/24087 TrojanOrders checkout exploitation from known attacker IP · [LLM] Magento php-fpm/web-server spawning shell or download utility (CVE-2022-24086 RCE)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Magento webshell drop (health_check.php / pub-media PHP) written by web-server process
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Outbound or inbound connection to TrojanOrders C2/source IP 45.134.20.11 · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Magento CVE-2022-24086/24087 TrojanOrders checkout exploitation from known attacker IP AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound web requests associated with TrojanOrders exploitation of the Magento checkout template-injection RCE (CVE-2022-24086/24087), pivoting on the Sansec-published attacker source IP 45.134.20.11 and POSTs to checkout/guest-cart REST endpoints that carry the malicious mail-template directives. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact CVEs and the confirmed campaign source IP rather than generic web exploitation. Rationale: Anchors on CVE-2022-24086/24087, the exact Magento checkout/guest-cart REST endpoints, the template-engine functions getTemplateFilter/addAfterFilterCallback abused for RCE, and the Sansec-confirmed campaign source IP 45.134.20.11 (corroborated by a second source). Cross-checked against: • https://sansec.io/research/magento-2-cve-2022-24086 • https://sansec.io/research/trojanorder-magento • https://labs.watchtowr.com/adobe-commerce-magento-rce-cve-2022-24086/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate guest checkout traffic from a shared NAT/proxy that happens to reuse the attacker IP range (rare)
  • Internal QA/load-testing tools POSTing to checkout REST endpoints
  • Vulnerability scanners (Tenable/Qualus) probing checkout endpoints during authorized scans
  • Payment provider callbacks hitting /rest/V1/carts with templated parameters
Suppression filter
| where not (csUserAgent has_any ("Nessus", "Qualys", "nuclei", "Tenable"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Pull all requests from 45.134.20.11 and any POST to checkout/guest-cart carrying {{ template directives; confirm Magento version is < 2.4.5-p1 / 2.3.7-p2 and whether MDVA-43395 + MDVA-43443 are both applied. Step 2 (contain): Block the source IP at WAF/edge, take the order-confirmation mail queue offline, and isolate the web node if a 2xx response was returned to a directive payload. Step 3 (hunt): Search the webroot (pub/media, var/) for newly written or modified .php files, especially health_check.php, and review php-fpm child processes (see related UCs).
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csMethod == "POST"
| where cIP == "45.134.20.11"
   or csUriStem has_any ("/checkout", "/rest/V1/guest-carts", "/V1/carts", "/customer/address")
   or csUriQuery has_any ("getTemplateFilter", "addAfterFilterCallback", "{{var", "{{config", "{{trans")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, csUserAgent, scStatus, TimeTaken
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Magento webshell drop (health_check.php / pub-media PHP) written by web-server process AIInstallation High
Hunts the TrojanOrders post-exploitation step where the PHP/web-server process writes a backdoor — frequently disguised as the legitimate Magento health_check.php or a stray .php under pub/media or var/ — providing persistent RAT access. Article-specific to the CVE-2022-24086 RCE chain rather than generic file-write monitoring. Rationale: Uses the corroborated TrojanOrders TTP that the backdoor is hidden in the legitimate health_check.php and that web shells land under pub/media or var, scoped to writes initiated by php-fpm/web-server processes (high-fidelity parent context). Cross-checked against: • https://sansec.io/research/trojanorder-magento • https://sansec.io/research/magento-2-cve-2022-24086
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Magento deploy/setup:upgrade regenerating cache/generated PHP under var/
  • Composer plugin installs writing PHP into module directories
  • Legitimate redeploy of the genuine health_check.php during patching
  • CMS plugin that legitimately renders user-uploaded templates to pub/media
  • Backup/restore jobs rehydrating PHP files into the webroot
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "setup:upgrade" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "setup:di:compile"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Hash the written .php and diff it against the stock Magento health_check.php / vendor copy; any eval/base64/system/assert content confirms a web shell. Step 2 (contain): Quarantine the file, isolate the web node, and rotate Magento admin + DB + integration credentials reachable from that host. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot on the writing php-fpm PID for child shell processes and outbound connections, and search all other web nodes for the same filename/hash and for the attacker IP 45.134.20.11.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

Magento php-fpm/web-server spawning shell or download utility (CVE-2022-24086 RCE) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the command-execution stage of the TrojanOrders chain: a Magento PHP/web-server process spawning sh/bash/curl/wget/python to run attacker commands or pull a RAT after the checkout template-injection RCE. Article-specific because the parent set is the Magento PHP runtime tied to the named CVEs. Rationale: Pairs the Magento PHP/web-server parent (the runtime that interprets the malicious mail template per Synacktiv's CVE-2022-24086 analysis) with shell/downloader children — the combination that turns the RCE into command execution and RAT retrieval. Cross-checked against: • https://www.synacktiv.com/en/publications/magento-template-engine-a-story-of-cve-2022-24086 • https://sansec.io/research/trojanorder-magento
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Magento cron (php bin/magento cron:run) invoking shell helpers for image processing
  • ImageMagick/exec-based media resizing spawning convert via sh
  • Composer or deployment scripts running curl/wget from a PHP wrapper
  • Monitoring agents invoking php that shells out to system tools
  • Legitimate cache-warmer scripts calling curl against the storefront
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has "bin/magento cron:run" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "cron:run"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Inspect the child ProcessCommandLine for base64, curl/wget to an external host, or reverse-shell patterns; correlate the parent php-fpm PID timing with checkout web requests. Step 2 (contain): Kill the offending process tree, isolate the node, and block any external host the downloader contacted. Step 3 (hunt): Enumerate files dropped by the child process, check for the health_check.php web shell, and sweep other web nodes for the same parent→child pattern and the 45.134.20.11 IP.

Outbound or inbound connection to TrojanOrders C2/source IP 45.134.20.11 AICommand & Control Medium

ATT&CKT1071.001 T1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment

  • Threat-intel/sandbox detonation hosts deliberately contacting the IP
  • Security researcher reproduction traffic from a test box
  • Shared CDN/cloud reassignment causing unrelated traffic to the same address
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Determine direction — inbound to a web node (exploitation attempt) vs outbound from a server (likely compromise/callback) — and identify the initiating process. Step 2 (contain): Block 45.134.20.11 at the perimeter; if the connection was outbound from a Magento host, isolate it pending web-shell review. Step 3 (hunt): Confirm Magento patch level (MDVA-43395 + MDVA-43443), search webroot for health_check.php and stray pub/media PHP, and review php-fpm child-process activity around the connection time.

Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.

Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.

DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Magento CVE-2022-24086 template-directive injection in web requests (getTemplateFilter RCE) AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound HTTP requests carrying the CVE-2022-24086/24087 Magento template-engine injection payload (the {{var this.getTemplateFilter().addAfterFilterCallback(system).filter(...)}} pattern) against checkout/wishlist endpoints. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact directive method names the exploit abuses rather than generic SSTI. Rationale: Anchored on the exact exploit method names (getTemplateFilter / addAfterFilterCallback / resetAfterFilterCallbacks) from the public CVE-2022-24086 PoC, confirmed by Sansec and Synacktiv — these strings appear in no legitimate Magento HTTP traffic, giving very low FP. Cross-checked against: • https://sansec.io/research/magento-2-cve-2022-24086 • https://www.synacktiv.com/en/publications/magento-template-engine-a-story-of-cve-2022-24086 • https://labs.watchtowr.com/adobe-commerce-magento-rce-cve-2022-24086/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized vulnerability scanners (Snyk, Qualys, Nessus) replaying CVE-2022-24086 test payloads
  • Internal pentest / red-team validation of Magento patch status
  • Security researchers' PoC repos being curl-tested from a corporate jump box
  • WAF in detection-only mode logging blocked-but-passed-through probe traffic
Suppression filter
| where cIP !in ("<vuln-scanner-ips>") // maintain scanner allowlist; remove if not applicable
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the targeted host runs Magento/Adobe Commerce and check its version against the patched 2.4.3-p1 / 2.3.7-p2 baseline; pull the full request body and scStatus (200 vs 4xx/5xx indicates success vs block). 2) Contain: block the source cIP at the WAF/edge, and if a 2xx response was returned to an injection request, isolate the web host pending RCE review. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC2 (php/w3wp spawning shells) on the same host and timeframe, and to UC3 (45.134.20.11) for related infrastructure; review newly written files in pub/, var/, and any PHP under web-accessible dirs for webshells.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csUriStem has_any ("getTemplateFilter","addAfterFilterCallback","resetAfterFilterCallbacks")
   or csUriQuery has_any ("getTemplateFilter","addAfterFilterCallback","resetAfterFilterCallbacks","%7B%7Bvar","{{var")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Magento web process (w3wp/php-fpm/php-cgi) spawning OS shell — CVE-2022-24086 RCE AIExploitation High
Detects the post-exploitation stage of CVE-2022-24086 where the injected template callback executes a system command, causing the Magento web/PHP worker to spawn a command interpreter or recon binary (the PoC runs system(id)). Article-specific via the PHP/web-server parent → shell child chain on a Magento host. Rationale: The published PoC runs system(id) via the template callback, so a Magento web/PHP worker parenting a shell or id/whoami/uname is the direct execution artifact of CVE-2022-24086 — w3wp/php-fpm legitimately never spawn interactive shells. Cross-checked against: • https://sansec.io/research/magento-2-cve-2022-24086 • https://vovohelo.medium.com/reversing-a-magento-rce-cve-2022-24086-e991ead4d8af
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004 T1059.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Composer/Magento CLI deploy jobs (bin/magento) invoking php that shells out to git/curl during build
  • Cron-driven Magento maintenance tasks launching sh wrappers
  • Monitoring agents (New Relic, Datadog) instrumenting php-fpm and forking shell health checks
  • Sysadmin running php-based diagnostics from the web user account
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("bin/magento","setup:upgrade","composer","deploy") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "cron")
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the child ProcessCommandLine — id/whoami/uname/curl with no scheduled-task or deploy-pipeline parent is high-confidence RCE; correlate the timestamp with UC1 injection hits in W3CIISLog. 2) Contain: isolate the web host, kill the offending process tree, and rotate any DB/admin credentials reachable from app config (env.php). 3) Hunt: enumerate files written by the web user in pub/media, var/, app/ for dropped webshells; check for new admin users, cron entries, and outbound connections to 45.134.20.11 (UC3).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("w3wp.exe","php-cgi.exe","php.exe","php-fpm","httpd","nginx","apache2")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","sh","bash","dash","id","whoami","whoami.exe","uname","curl","wget")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("w3wp.exe","php-cgi.exe","php.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","whoami.exe","net.exe","curl.exe","certutil.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Network activity involving CVE-2022-24086 attacker IP 45.134.20.11 AICommand & Control Medium
Alerts on any inbound or outbound network/web activity involving 45.134.20.11, the source IP Sansec attributed to in-the-wild CVE-2022-24086 Magento exploitation. Narrow, high-fidelity IOC pivot for confirming the campaign touched the estate. Rationale: 45.134.20.11 is the discrete attacker source IP both the article IOC set and Sansec independently tie to active CVE-2022-24086 exploitation; a single named IPv4 is a clean, low-FP alerting pivot. Cross-checked against: • https://sansec.io/research/magento-2-cve-2022-24086 • https://www.wiz.io/vulnerability-database/cve/cve-2022-24086
ATT&CKT1190 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IP later re-allocated to benign hosting after the 2022 campaign (verify the hit timeframe)
  • Threat-intel platform or honeypot deliberately connecting to the IOC for enrichment
  • Security team running their own connectivity test against the indicator
  • Shared-hosting NAT egress causing an unrelated tenant to reuse the address
Response runbook
1) Triage: determine direction — inbound to an internet-facing Magento host implies exploitation attempt; outbound from a web host implies post-compromise callback (more severe). Correlate with UC1/UC2 hits on the same asset. 2) Contain: block 45.134.20.11 at the perimeter in both directions and snapshot the involved host before remediation. 3) Hunt: sweep all web logs for the UC1 injection strings from this IP, review for dropped webshells and new admin accounts, and confirm the host's Magento version is patched to 2.4.3-p1/2.3.7-p2 with both MDVA-43395 and MDVA-43443 applied.

| where RemoteIP == "45.134.20.11" or LocalIP == "45.134.20.11"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, LocalIP, LocalPort, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
union isfuzzy=true
(CommonSecurityLog
 | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
 | where SourceIP == "45.134.20.11" or DestinationIP == "45.134.20.11"
 | project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, SourceIP, DestinationIP, DestinationPort, RequestURL, DeviceAction),
(W3CIISLog
 | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
 | where cIP == "45.134.20.11"
 | project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

2022-02-16 1 use case 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

Use the Snyk Vulnerability database to help you find an opportunity to make an open source contribution to The Big Fix!

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,

          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
